News

Market analysis

OIL FUTURES: Crude little changed as market eyes US stock draw, slowing Hormuz transits
US crude inventories fall 3.06 million barrels
Cushing stocks build for fifth straight week
Hormuz ship traffic drops to 16 vessels
Crude prices ended the session little changed Oct. 21, as supply concerns remained in focus amid continued US inventory draws and falling Strait of Hormuz traffic.
NYMEX December WTI settled down 67 cents/barrel at $97.15/b, and ICE December Brent climbed 3 cents/b to end the session at $108.82/b.
US commercial crude stocks declined 3.06 million barrels to 412.52 million barrels during the week to Oct. 16, US Energy Information Administration data showed Oct. 21. This third straight weekly draw put stocks at the lowest outright level since late July and 3.7% behind the EIA's seasonal five-year average.
But stockpiles at the NYMEX delivery hub of Cushing, Oklahoma, climbed for a fifth straight week, moving 990,000 barrels higher to 27.59 million barrels.
The topline draw comes as weekly production slid 3% to 13.27 million barrels/day, putting nationwide output at the lowest since late January. This slowdown is due in large part to Gulf of Mexico shut-ins ahead of Hurricane Isaias, which at peak shut in approximately 1.459 million b/d or 71.5% of Gulf of Mexico crude output, according to US Bureau of Safety and Environmental Enforcement data.
NYMEX November RBOB climbed 33 points/gallon to $3.1205/ gal and November ULSD declined 16.71 cents/gal to $4.7633/gal.

International Crude
SAUDI DATA: August Saudi crude output at record low, exports climb: JODI
August output falls to 6.238 million b/d
Monthly exports second-highest since start of war
Storage withdrawals highest on JODI records
Saudi Arabian crude production fell to a record low in August, while exports were at their second highest since the regional war's outbreak, according to data published by the Joint Organizations Data Initiative on Oct. 21.
Saudi crude output stood at 6.238 million b/d in August, compared with September's wartime high of 8.135 million b/d, according to JODI data, returning to early-war production levels, which averaged 6.614 million b/d over the first three months of the Iran conflict, according to JODI.
On July 20, Yemeni Houthi militias declared a maritime blockade on all Saudi-linked shipping through the Bab al-Mandab Strait. Since then, transits through the Red Sea via the strait have grown riskier, prompting more northward transits via the Suez Canal and Mediterranean re-exports of Saudi crude.
Saudi Arabia's crude exports were 4.451 million b/d in August, JODI data showed, up from 4.125 million b/d in July, as constrained Bab al-Mandeb shipping flows necessitated redirections amid the Houthi blockade imposed in late July.
August export levels stand as the second-highest since the start of the US-Israel war with Iran in late February, according to JODI data. March remains the highest wartime export volume.
Saudi Arabia also imported 68,000 b/d of crude in the month, climbing to the highest level since February.
Saudi Arabia drew 30.435 million barrels, or just over 980,000 b/d, from its stockpiles over August, the highest stock draw on JODI records. This follows a record stock build in July of 31.422 million barrels, or roughly 1 million b/d added to worldwide storage facilities, JODI data showed.
The country used 604,000 b/d of crude for power generation and in other industries during the peak summer demand month of August, remaining at the same level as the previous year's crude burn volumes for the month, JODI data showed.
Saudi refinery intake stood at 2.297 million b/d in August, according to JODI, down month over month and the lowest level since April.
-- Lulwa Albuainain

TURKEY DATA: Iraq-Turkey crude pipeline flows up 72% in September
ITP flows average 261,800 b/d in Sept
Ceyhan-Kirikkale flows down up 19% from July
BTC flows up 10% on month
Crude oil flows through the Iraq-Turkey pipeline (ITP) totaled 7.854 million barrels in September, averaging 261,800 b/d, data published by Turkey's state pipeline operator, Botas, showed Oct. 20.
The average daily flow was up 71.9% compared to August, when flows were the lowest recorded since March, when the pipeline was halted for a period during negotiations between the Iraqi government and oil producers in Iraq's Kurdistan region.
Monthly and daily flows for September were the highest reported since flow restarted in September 2025, and were 16.3% up on the previous highest flow of 217,433 b/d reported in November 2025.
The sudden sharp rise follows the agreement between Turkey and Iraq signed Aug. 1, under which operation of the line will continue under the existing ITP inter-governmental operating agreement for a further year, pending the conclusion of an agreement for a new operating agreement, and other agreements on possible cross-border gas and power trading.
Under that agreement, Baghdad undertook to increase ITP flow to about 1 million b/d but did not specify when it expected that level to be reached.
Flow through the line has been limited since restarting in late September 2025, with producers in Iraq's Kurdistan region preferring to sell the bulk of their output to local refiners instead of to Iraq's state oil marketing authority, SOMO, for export.
In addition to volumes from Kurdish producers, which are reaching 1 million b/d, that level will require substantial output from Iraq's northern Kirkuk oil fields, which are estimated to hold about 3 billion barrels of reserves and are being refurbished by BP under a March 2025 contract with Baghdad.
Subsequent agreements saw ConocoPhillips take a 42% stake in the project and Turkey's state upstream operator TPAO 15%.
Turkey's energy minister Alparslan Bayraktar said Oct. 18 that TPAO would begin to receive its share from the country's Kirkuk oilfields in 2027.
The ITP consists of two parallel strings with a total capacity of about 2.4 million b/d. The Iraqi section of the larger pipeline, with a nameplate capacity of 1.5 million b/d, was damaged during the Islamic State insurgency in the 2010s and is undergoing rehabilitation.
Flows from Ceyhan via the Ceyhan-Kirikkale pipeline to Tupras's 108,000 b/d Kirikkale refinery in September were 111,133 b/d, down 4.3% from August and up 27.3% year over year.
The Kirikkale refinery was designed to be supplied with Iraqi crude arriving at Ceyhan via the ITP, the reopening of which had been expected to see the plant return to refining primarily Iraqi crude.

Tupras has not confirmed how much crude arriving at Ceyhan via the ITP is being supplied to Kirikkale following the line's reopening.
According to the latest official Turkish data, in August, Tupras imported 183,438 mt of Iraqi crude via the ITP, down 39.5% from July.
BTC flows rise Flows of Azeri crude to Ceyhan through the Baku-Tbilisi-
Ceyhan pipeline totaled 16.078 million barrels in September, or an average of 535,933 b/d, up 9.9% from August when flow was down 4.6% month over month.
The 1 million b/d BTC pipeline mainly carries crude produced by the BP-operated ACG oil fields together with volumes of Azeri crude from fields operated by Azerbaijan's state oil company Socar, and volumes of crude from operators in Kazakhstan and Turkmenistan.
On Aug. 19, BP announced it had started a planned 11-day maintenance program on the Central Azeri platform, which it said would reduce output from the ACG field during the period.
-- David O'Byrne
KPC in talks with `strategic allies' to move hydrocarbons through pipelines: CEO
KPC pursues pipeline deals with Gulf allies
Kuwaiti oil output reaches 2 million b/d
Gulf crude shows lower carbon than US oil
Kuwait Petroleum Corp. is in talks with "strategic allies" in the Persian Gulf about moving hydrocarbons, including crude oil, through pipelines, and progress is "extremely positive," CEO Shaikh al-Sabah said Oct. 21.
"We're in the engineering phases of some of them," Sabah told the Dow Jones Energy Forum. "We're very serious about moving it through. And even the financing is going to be international efforts."
KPC was in talks with Saudi Arabia and the UAE on pipeline and port options and was looking to increase domestic storage and stocks at its overseas refineries, including in Japan and South Korea, Platts reported earlier this month. Sabah noted at the time that concerns were growing around refined product supply shortages and elevated prices.
KPC estimated that around 6 million b/d of refined products previously produced in the Gulf have left the market. Before the war, Kuwait was Europe's biggest jet fuel supplier and the second-largest diesel supplier.
KPC is also looking for foreign investment in projects, including pipelines and offshore fields, the CEO said.
In July, KPC announced that its wholly-owned Kuwait Oil Co., responsible for crude and pipelines within Kuwait, signed a $16 billion lease and lease-back agreement involving its domestic and export pipeline network for 20.5 years with a consortium of international infrastructure and institutional investors

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 21, 2026

Blackstone, Brookfield and KKR. That agreement had been agreed to on Feb. 27, one day
before the Iran war began, so it was delayed to allow investors to understand the new outlook, Sabah said.
It ended up being Kuwait's largest energy infrastructure partnership at the time and the largest foreign direct investment ever in Kuwait, according to the statement of the deal. It was the first time global investors put capital into Kuwait's midstream infrastructure, according to the company.
The investment was in line with KPC's plans to reach 4 million barrels of crude production per day by 2035, according to the statement.
A 50% increase in energy demand expected by 2050 cannot be met by renewables and every other source of energy unless crude oil is included, and crudes from the Persian Gulf have lower carbon intensity than crudes in the US, Sabah said at the Oct. 21 forum.
"Any way you look at it, energy demand is increasing," he said. Maybe 75% of energy demand can be met by the other sources, he said. "So then the question becomes whose oil is it going to be." Each barrel of crude from the Gulf is associated with about 5 kilograms of CO2, he said. In the US, by comparison, the carbon intensity of shale is closer to 15 kg to 20 kg per barrel, and deepwater crude is well over 30 kg per barrel, he said.
"We have the lowest cost barrel. We have the lowest carbon intensity barrel. And that's where the pressure, I think, will come in," he said. "It's not just on cost, it's on carbon intensity."
-- Claudia Carpenter

CRUDE MOC: Middle East crude differentials edge higher
Platts Dubai, Oman premiums up $1.33/b DOD
10 Dubai partials trade during MOC

Cash differentials for Middle East sour crude markers edged higher during the Singapore Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 21.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed December cash Dubai and cash Oman both at a premium of $5.54/b to samemonth Dubai futures at the market close, up $1.33/b day over day.
December cash Murban was up 59 cents/b over the same period at a premium of $6.50/b to same-month Dubai futures.
During the MOC, 10 December Dubai partials of 25,000 barrels each traded. The sellers were Glencore, Mitsui, and Unipec, while the buyers were Trafigura and ExxonMobil.
No convergences were reached during the MOC. A convergence occurs when 20 partials are traded between two counterparties in

Platts Asia Pacific Sweet Crude Daily Market Analysis
PTTEP sells Dec-loading barrels of Kimanis crude to PTT
Tupi crude into China heard traded for January
Russian Sokol crude spot trading activity thin: trader
The Asia-Pacific sweet crude market on Oct. 21 saw the first North West Shelf cargo for the December trading cycle change hands, along with further trade clarity on PTTEP's Kimanis crude sell tender.
On the condensate segment, Chevron was heard to have moved its Dec. 4-8 loading barrels of the key Australian North West Shelf condensate, according to various crude sources; however, further price levels of the trade remain unascertained.
The second cargo of North West Shelf condensate for the December trading cycle, set to be loaded over Dec. 15-19, is in the hands of Australian producer Woodside Energy.
Meanwhile, on the medium-sweet crude front, Thailand's state-owned PTT Exploration & Production was heard to have sold Dec. 15-19 loading barrels of the Malaysian Kimanis crude cargo to PTT; however, further trade details remain unascertained.
Valuations for December-loading barrels of Malaysian Miri Light crude and Labuan crude were heard at premiums in the low-$20s/b and mid-$20s/b to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, FOB, according to a regional crude trader.
The Platts-assessed second-month gasoil and jet fuel swap cracks against Dubai crude swaps averaged $ 64.76/b and $67.47/b, respectively, as of the Oct. 21 Asian close, compared with averages of $72.97/b and $73.39/b in September.
Over on the arbitrage crude front, trades were heard on the Brazilian crude front, with January-arrival barrels of Tupi crude being heard to have changed hands at a premium in the $10/b to Platts Dated Brent crude assessments, DES China, trending much lower than initial offers for the grade, which had been

Platts North Sea Crude Daily Market Analysis
North Sea differentials rebound; TA freight stabilizes
Backwardation widens further along Dated Brent strip
North Sea crude oil differentials rebounded Oct. 21 with prices finding a floor amid stabilizing transatlantic freight and a steepening backwardation.
WTI Midland cargoes continued to change hands during the Platts Market on Close assessment process Oct. 21.
This marked the third traded WTI Midland cargo in the North Sea market since the start of the week - as many as the whole of the previous week.
Glencore lifted an Occidental offer for a WTI Midland cargo arriving in Rotterdam over Nov. 21-25 at a premium of $10.65/b over Dated Brent.
Meanwhile, Vitol sought a WTI Midland cargo arriving in Rotterdam over Nov. 16-20 at a premium of $10.95/barrel over Dated Brent, while Eni bid for a WTI Midland cargo arriving over Nov. 6-10 at a premium of $10/b over Dated Brent.
Both bids were left outstanding at the London close. Transatlantic freight rates continued to stabilize following a recent sell-off that traders cited as a key drag on landed differentials of the US grade. Platts assessed the US Gulf Coast-UK/Continent Aframax

freight rate at $136.57/metric ton Oct. 20, unchanged day over day.
Nevertheless, the broader freight environment remained relatively expensive, one North Sea crude trade cautioned.
"Everybody's shouting how freight paper is cheap and it's w500," the trader said, referring to the cross-UKC Worldscale freight rate.
At the same time, backwardation along the North Sea Dated Brent strip extended its rebound Oct. 21.
Platts assessed the Week 2 Brent CFD contract at a $7.28/b premium over its Week 6 counterpart, widening $1.77/b on the day and $3.96/b from the start of the week.
The regional sour segment remained hampered by a wellsupplied environment in Europe, owing to ample cargo availability and competing barrels pointed toward the region.
"I think Guyana and other Latin American grades [competing into Europe] plus ample Middle Eastern avails are pressuring things," a sour crude trader said.
Additionally, Iran has opened "special routes" for Iraqi ships through the Strait of Hormuz to help Iraq mitigate the cost of the ongoing crisis in the waterway, Tehran said Oct. 20.
Platts is part of S&P Global Energy. -- Ernest Puey
Platts West African Crude Daily Commentary
Market remains bearish, adds pressure to differentials
Demand stable, despite low Chinese activity
Stabilization continued in the West African crude complex on Oct. 21 as freight rates softened further, though bearish pressure continued to weigh on differentials, according to market sources.
Although recent high freight costs continued to ease on the day, players in the market remained mostly bearish, noting uncertainty about how significant the softer freight rates would prove to be, and whether any recovery in differential values could be sustained.
"Freight easing into this week may provide some WAF relief, but nothing substantial," one WAF trader told Platts. "The general market [feeling is that] as Asian refiners are reducing demand for African and US crude, WAF will keep discounting."
"Differentials will spike once a Suezmax relief comes, but then will immediately floor again," the trader added. "I think once relief is heard and there is more movement in the [Middle East], people will ramp differentials up, only to realize the appetite isn't there."
Nevertheless, current demand was said to remain relatively stable. "Fundamentally, there is good demand," a second WAF trader said. "Some technical things, such as year-end inventories, could have some impacts on December loaders, but it should not be a big issue."
However, it was heard that recent Chinese buying activity of West African crudes was still low, cutting out some demand. Traders had mixed views on this, with some attributing it to high freight costs, while others saw it as an additional driver pushing

Americas Crude
US crude stocks hit 11-week low after Isaias shuts in Gulf of Mexico production
SPR draws resume, stocks hit fresh 44-year low
Cushing stocks build for fifth week
Hurricane Isaias shut-ins trim US output by 3%
US commercial crude stocks fell to 11-week lows during the week to Oct. 16, US Energy Information Administration data showed Oct. 21, as Hurricane Isaias-related production shut-ins weighed on output.
Commercial crude stocks declined 3.06 million barrels to 412.52 million barrels during the week, EIA data showed. This third straight weekly draw put stocks at the lowest outright level since

late July and 3.7% behind the EIA's seasonal five-year average. A further 300,000 barrels were drained from the nation's
Strategic Petroleum Reserve, putting total SPR volumes down to a fresh 44-year low of 282.68 million barrels.
Inventory draws were seen mostly on the US Gulf Coast, where stocks declined 5.04 million barrels to a 22-month low of 229.32 million barrels.
Notably, stockpiles at the NYMEX delivery hub of Cushing, Oklahoma, climbed for a fifth straight week, moving 990,000 barrels higher to 27.59 million barrels.
The topline draw comes as weekly production slid 3% to 13.27 million barrels/day, putting nationwide output at the lowest since late January.
This slowdown is due in large part to Gulf of Mexico shut-ins ahead of Hurricane Isaias, which tracked northward through the central Gulf of Mexico before making landfall as a category 2 hurricane near Destin, Florida late Oct. 9. At peak on Oct. 9, approximately 1.459 million b/d of crude and 1.26 million cubic feet/day of gas production was offline, US Bureau of Safety and Environmental Enforcement data showed, respectively representing 71.5% and 59% of total Gulf of Mexico output. Shutins extended well into the reporting period, with the last update from BSEE on Oct. 14 showing approximately 10% of Gulf of Mexico crude production and 11% of gas output remained offline.
While Isaias significantly curtailed crude production, the storm tracked east of the bulk of USGC refinery infrastructure and there was little impact on refinery runs. Total US refinery utilization declined 0.1 percentage point to 93.4% of capacity, EIA data showed, holding 6.7% above the seasonal five-year average. USGC refinery runs slowed to 95.9% of capacity, a decline of 0.4 percentage point week over week.
Nationwide distillate inventories climbed 1.68 million barrels to 104.29 million barrels, EIA data showed. The build snapped four straight weeks of inventory draws and narrowed the deficit to the five-year average to 9% from 11.1% the week prior. Still, distillate stocks set a fresh seasonal five-year low for the period and remain 11.27 million barrels behind year-ago levels.
Gasoline stocks declined 2.95 million barrels to 205.31 million barrels. The draw also left stocks at a fresh five-year low for the period and put them 4.7% behind the five-year average for this time of year.

Venezuelan imports rise 116,000 b/d weekly

US imports of Latin American and Caribbean crude were mostly mixed in the week ended Oct. 16, with incoming volumes of Guyanese crude falling to a six-week low, according to data released by the US Energy Information Administration on Oct. 21.
US imports of Guyanese crude reached a five-week low of 80,000 b/d of crude in the week ended Oct. 16, down 234,000 b/d week over week. This brought down the four-week average by 23,000 b/d to 228,000 b/d.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, last assessed Guyana's flagship Liza crude at a $28/b discount to the Latin American Dated Brent strip on Oct. 20.
The US imported 571,000 barrels/day of crude from neighboring Venezuela in the most recent reporting week, up 116,000 b/d from the previous week. However, the four-week average inched lower to 551,000 b/d, reflecting lower import averages over the last two weeks when they previously exceeded 600,000 b/d from the week ended July 17 to the week ended Oct. 2.
Platts last assessed Venezuela's Merey 16 and Hamaca crudes on a delivered-at-place basis to the US Gulf Coast at discounts of $31.75/barrel and $30.75/b, respectively, to the Latin American Brent Futures strip on Oct. 20.
In the most recent reporting week, the US imported 154,000 b/d of Mexican crude, down 59,000 b/d from the previous week, the lowest level in the three weeks since the 144,000 b/d imported the week ended Sept. 25.
Meanwhile, the US imported 122,000 b/d of crude from Colombia, down 133,000 b/d week over week, while the four-week average lifted to 130,000 b/d.
Platts last assessed Colombia's Vasconia and Castilla crudes at discounts of $15/b and $30.20/b to the Latin American Brent Futures, respectively, on Oct. 20.
The US imports of Brazilian crude rose by 43,000 b/d to 183,000

b/d. The four-week average fell for the second consecutive week to 281,000 b/d, the lowest level since the week ended Sept. 18.
Platts assessed Brazil's Tupi crude at a $19.60/b discount to the Latin American Dated Brent strip on Oct. 20.
From Argentina, the US imported 100,000 b/d of Argentine barrels after not importing any barrels the previous week. The four-week average softened to 87,000 b/d.
Platts last assessed Argentina's Medanito at a $7.50/b discount to the Latin American Brent Futures strip on Oct. 20.

US DATA: Canadian crude imports reach five-week high, EIA says
Canadian crude inflows jump 151,000 b/d on week
Four-week average climbs to 3.74 million b/d
US imports of Canadian crude rose to a five-week high in the week ended Oct. 16, according to data published by the US Energy Information Administration on Oct. 21.
Canadian crude flows into the US climbed by 151,000 barrels/ day in the most recent reporting week, reaching 3.9 million b/d, the highest level of imports since the Sept. 11 reporting week. US imports of Canadian crude remained 333,000 b/d higher than the same period in 2025, with imports in the Oct. 17, 2025, reporting week totaling 3.57 million b/d.
After falling to a near one-year low the previous reporting week, Canadian crude oil imports rebounded by 162,000 b/d on a four-week basis for the period ended Oct. 16, 2026. The four-week average for Canadian crude imports was 3.74 million b/d, the highest since the Sept. 18 reporting week.
Despite the weekly build, the four-week average for Canadian crude imports was 17,000 b/d lower than in the Oct. 17, 2025, reporting week, which was 3.76 million b/d, the EIA data showed.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, last assessed Western Canadian Select crude at Hardisty, Alberta, at a $28.15/barrel discount to the West Texas Intermediate CMA on Oct. 20, down 25 cents/b day over day.

Canadian pipelines see higher apportionment, heavy oil price discounts widen
Mainline apportionment set at 16% for November
TMX sets record 7% apportionment
Growing Asian demand for Canadian crude
Operators, Enbridge and Trans Mountain Corp., have unveiled higher apportionment for their crude oil pipelines from the Western Canadian Sedimentary Basin to the US Gulf Coast and the Pacific Coast, primarily driven by rising demand, even as price differentials for heavy barrels continue to widen, they said separately.
For the Mainline system, the first point of apportionment for heavy crude is 16% at Superior (covering Lines 6 and 61) and

Platts Oilgram Price Report

October 21, 2026

7% at Kerrobert (for Lines 4, 67 and 93) as the second point of apportionment, Enbridge spokesperson Gina Sutherland told Platts in an email Oct. 21.
For light crude, the first point of apportionment is 19% at Kerrobert (for Lines 2 and 93) and 13% at Cromer for Lines 2, 93 and 65 as the second point of apportionment, Sutherland said.
In October, lights and heavies have been apportioned at 8% and 4%, respectively, both at Kerrobert, she said, indicating a single point of entry to the pipeline for the month.
Enbridge had fully removed apportionment on the Mainline for September, marking it the first time in 2026 that the Calgarybased operator had made such a move, Sutherland said then, adding a reason for that was the seasonal industry maintenance by Alberta's heavy oil and oil sands producers.
The over 3 million barrels/day, 8,600-mile Mainline is the single-largest oil pipeline connecting the WCSB to markets in the US and ships both heavy and light crude directly and indirectly from Alberta to refineries and storage hubs in the US Midwest, Eastern Canada, and the USGC through multiple pipelines that are part of the overall system.
Heavy oil apportionment on the pipeline system has been high since January, when it was 13% for both light and heavy barrels, rising to 18% for light and 19% for heavy crude in June, Enbridge data showed.
Despite the high levels of apportionment, producers have been nominating incremental barrels for shipment on the Mainline, with throughput volumes averaging 3.1 million b/d in the second quarter of 2026, Sutherland said.
TMX seen moving more barrels to Asia Meanwhile, apportionment for the 890,000-b/d Trans Mountain
Pipeline system for October stands at 7%, TMC said on its website. The apportionment is the third time this year, with levels at
0.2% in June and an unspecified percentage in July, TMC said at the time.
The pipeline system that runs from Edmonton, Alberta, to the Westridge Marine Terminal at Burnaby in coastal British Columbia comprises the legacy 300,000 b/d Trans Mountain pipeline and the 590,000 b/d Trans Mountain Expansion, which was started up in May 2024, has since loaded 691 vessels at the terminal, with more than 65% (or 455 vessels) headed to Asia, TMC said on its website, noting the pipeline was 90% full in September.
"China has been at the forefront of sourcing WCS, AWB and Canadian dilbit being delivered by TMX," Greg Stringham, a former vice president for markets with the Canadian Association of Petroleum Producers, said separately. "But of late the [South] Koreans have significantly stepped up, followed by the Japanese. This will result in higher apportionment levels in the coming months as WCSB producers are set to increase output, particularly in the winter season."
Western Canadian Select and Access Western Blend are two primary grades of heavy barrels in the WCSB.
Canadian crude oil production (including pentanes and condensate) is anticipated to reach 5.5 million b/d in 2026, 5.8

million b/d in 2027 and 6 million b/d in 2028, which is a growth of nearly 450,000 b/d in three years, S&P Global Energy CERA said in its latest monthly report.
WCS prices remain weak WCS at Hardisty, Alberta, and at Nederland, Texas, has
declined significantly since Sept. 24, with WCS Hardisty falling by $7.50/b and WCS Nederland by $8.00/b.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed WCS Hardisty and WCS Nederland at discounts of $28.00/b and $19.50/b Oct. 21, respectively, to the WTI CMA, both strengthening by 15 cents/b from the day before. Despite narrowing differentials on the day, WCS Hardisty and WCS Nederland are hovering at their lowest levels since Nov. 2023 and Dec. 2022, respectively.
Market sources told Platts that oversupply and increased competition from other heavy crudes into the US Gulf Coast, such as Venezuela's Merey 16, have put pressure on WCS's differentials.
"I think people are starting to realize how bad WCS could be going forward," one Canadian crude oil trader said, "[There's] lots of competition in the Gulf, and I think up in Alberta, lots of supply around too."
On the West Coast, Pacific Cold Lake and Pacific Dilbit, two crudes transported on the TMX pipeline, hit a fresh all-time low every day the grades were assessed from Sept. 16 to Oct. 15. Pacific Cold Lake and Pacific Dilbit recorded their weakest differentials, at discounts of $24.40/b and $25.40/b, respectively.
Platts assessed Pacific Cold Lake and Pacific Dilbit at discounts of $23.15/b and $24.55/b, respectively, to the ICE Brent CMA, on Oct. 21, unchanged from the previous day.

Petroecuador to sell 16.2 million barrels of Oriente crude to Petroperu
$1.65 billion deal to supply Oriente crude over 11 months
Contract is first with Petroperu in four years
Petroecuador plans to drill 32 wells in Q4
Petroecuador announced a $1.65 billion contract to sell 16.2 million barrels of Oriente crude to Petroperu over the next 11 months, part of an ongoing drive to renew energy ties in the Andean region.
The contract, which will make Petroecuador the main supplier to Petroperu's underused 95,000 b/d Talara refinery, will supply 4.32 million barrels over a six-month period and 11.88 million barrels over 11 months, Ecuador's state oil company said late Oct. 20 in a statement. The contract may be extended to include additional crude supply, cooperation in logistics, maritime transport and storage, the company added.
Petroperu's Talara refinery, operating at 60% of capacity amid capital constraints, received the first $475 million tranche

of a $2 billion government bailout last month to finance crude purchases.
The contract, the first between Ecuador and Peru in four years, was announced during a visit to Peru by Ecuador's President Daniel Noboa and a joint Peru-Ecuador cabinet session. The move also comes after Ecuador and neighboring Colombia renewed an energy agreement, including pumping Colombian crude through Petroecuador's pipelines.
Petroecuador, that accounts for about 80% of the country's output, plans to drill 32 wells in the fourth quarter, part of a $180 million drilling program that aims to add 11,150 b/d in output, Ecuador's Energy Ministry said in a separate statement. The company drilled 95 wells and performed 245 well workovers through the first three quarters, the ministry added. Ecuador was producing 464,655 b/d through Oct. 19, according to the ministry.
-- Alex Emery
Ghana witnessing oil uptick, new upstream investment, president says
Oil output 'jumps 30%' in Ghana since 2025
Major oil companies 'knocking at the door'
Mahama focused on domestic value addition
Ghana's crude production has increased 30% since last year, with some of the world's largest oil and gas companies "knocking at the door" to invest in its upstream sector, the country's president, John Dramani Mahama, said Oct. 21.
Addressing the Financial Times Africa Summit in London, Mahama - who took office for the second time in January 2025 - said the West African country was committed to developing its natural resources for economic prosperity, including by encouraging new oil and mining investment and refining commodities from gold to cocoa domestically.
"There is an increased interest in our oil and gas prospects," Mahama said. "Chevron, Shell, ExxonMobil, everybody is knocking at the door to come and begin exploration."
He added: "Oil production has increased by 30% since last year."
Ghana became a rapid oil success story in the 2010s, achieving first oil in 2010 and growing production to 200,000 barrels/ day by 2019, according to officials, helping fund a slew of new infrastructure projects.
However, production slid in subsequent years due to limited investment, natural declines, the coronavirus pandemic and rig unavailability. Today it boasts just three producing oil assets in Jubilee, TEN and Sankofa.
A source at state-run Ghana National Petroleum Corp. told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, on Oct. 21 that the country produced 112,000 b/d of oil on Oct. 19.
Mahama's comments come one month after Ghana signed a memorandum of understanding with Italy's Eni and trading house Vitol that could lead to drilling on two offshore oil exploration blocks in the Tano Basin, while Brazil's state oil giant Petrobras is

Gasoline
European gasoline cracks fall to near 7-month low on winter demand lull
Gasoline crack spreads weakens to $19.50/b
Forward cracks stay elevated on year
High freight rates limit export arbitrage
European gasoline cracks fell to a near seven-month low Oct. 20 as winter seasonality weighed on demand, while elevated freight rates constrained export opportunities and added pressure to regional pricing.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the front-month E5 Eurobob crack at $19.50/barrel on Oct. 20, the lowest since March 31. The crack was $3.45/b weaker day over day but $5.35/b stronger year over year.
"Things [are] just softening a bit with the seasonality coming up on us," an European trader said. "I don't think there's a lot to read into it."
FOB ARA Eurobob barges were assessed at $1,123/mt on Oct. 20, down $56.50/mt day over day.
European refining margins have gradually converged following periods of wide intermonth spreads earlier in October and over the summer, as the market transitions into the seasonally weaker winter-demand period.
According to Platts pricing data, the M1/M2 crack spread differential narrowed to $1.89/b on Oct. 20, its lowest since midJune and down $7.62/b month over month. Further along the curve, the M2/M3 spread flipped to an 8 cents/b discount on Oct. 20, from a 61 cents/b premium the previous day.
High freight rates curb European gasoline export demand Adding pressure to European gasoline pricing, export demand
remained subdued, with high freight rates limiting arbitrage opportunities and keeping more volumes within the region.
"Freight is keeping volumes put," an Europe-based trader said, adding that "demand is low for both [oxy and non-oxy blends]."
Platts assessed clean tanker freight for 37,000 metric ton UKC-USAC cargoes at $30.15/mt on Oct. 20, up $1.55/mt day over day, while 37,000 mt UKC-WAF freight was assessed at $58.97/mt, up $4.47/mt day over day.
Paper arbitrage indications nevertheless strengthened Oct. 20. According to Intercontinental Exchange data, the November RBOB-EBOB differential widened 3.30 cents/gallon to 7.87 cents/ gal, while the December differential widened 0.63 cents/gal to 13.32 cents/gal.

European gasoline-naphtha spread hits 6-month low on firm LPG, thin gasoline demand
Gasoline-naphtha spread narrows 53% MOM as LPG prices rise
High freight rates limit gasoline export arbitrage
Petrochemical demand supports naphtha market
The European gasoline-naphtha spread fell to its lowest level since April as rising LPG prices improved naphtha's competitiveness as a petrochemical feedstock, while weak arbitrage economics limited gasoline export opportunities.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the front-month gasoline-naphtha spread at a six-month low of $146.25/metric ton on Oct. 20, $26/mt narrower day over day and 52.86% month over month.
European gasoline cracks fell to a near seven-month low on Oct. 20 as winter seasonality weighed on demand.
"Gasoline weakened significantly," an European trading source said.
Platts assessed the front-month E5 Eurobob crack at $19.50/ barrel on Oct. 20, its lowest since March 31. The crack was down $3.45/b day over day but remained $5.35/b higher year over year.
High freight rates curb gasoline export opportunities Adding pressure to European gasoline pricing, export demand
remained subdued, with high freight rates limiting arbitrage opportunities and keeping more volumes within the region.
"Freight is keeping volumes put," another Europe-based trader said, adding that "demand is low for both [oxy and non-oxy blends]."

Platts assessed clean tanker freight for 37,000 mt UKC-USAC cargoes at $30.15/mt on Oct. 20, up 25.83% month over month, while 37,000 mt UKC-WAF freight was assessed at $58.97/mt, up 83.31% over the same period.
Rising LPG prices boost naphtha feedstock competitiveness As gasoline blending demand for naphtha waned,
petrochemical buyers stepped back into the market, with rising LPG prices improving naphtha's competitiveness as an alternative feedstock.
"Hearing [the] market [is] finding some support from petchem," the same trading source said.
Propane prices have risen amid sluggish US exports and slow production in the North Sea, widening the propane-naphtha spread and making LPG less competitive as a feedstock for flexible steam crackers.
Meanwhile, a softer octane complex has weakened reforming margins, weighing on demand for heavy virgin naphtha.
"Reformer margins [are] down in line with lower octane value, so heavy virgin naphtha [and] full range naphtha [are] struggling," the trading source added.
Amid softer gasoline blending demand, reformate pricing out of the Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp hub fell $60.75/mt to $1,238.75/mt on Oct. 20, tracking weakness across Eurobob blends. The equivalent premium over the front-month E5 Eurobob swap fell $28.25/mt to $206/mt, nearing a one-month low and down $135.50/mt since the beginning of October.
"Octanes have corrected quite strongly," a gasoline trader said, adding that it was "hard to say where octanes are moving [to] at the moment," while agreeing that weaker reformer margins had weighed on naphtha demand.
Platts assessed the physical naphtha CIF NWE cargo price down $7.75/mt at $920.75/mt on Oct. 20, up $56/mt since Oct. 1, while the front-month naphtha crack was assessed at minus $4.85/b on the same day.

US DATA: East Coast gasoline imports fall to 23-week low, EIA says
Imports decrease 50% on week
Inventories fall 1.9 million barrels
Refinery output falls amid turnarounds
US East Coast gasoline imports dropped nearly 50% in the week ended Oct. 16 to a 23-week low, reducing supply into the Atlantic Basin and triggering a 1.915 million-barrel inventory draw, according to US Energy Information Administration data released on Oct. 21.
Gasoline imports into the East Coast fell 182,000 barrels/ day week over week to average just 188,000 b/d. The sharp drop marks the lowest weekly import rate for the Atlantic Coast since the week ended May 8, when imports averaged 166,000 b/d.
The import volume was also the lowest recorded in any October week since 2020. The week's average was 101,000 b/d lower than the year-ago week and stood at less than half of the 391,000 b/d imported during the same period in 2024.
The collapse in incoming waterborne cargoes was the primary driver of a sharp regional inventory drawdown during the most recent reporting week. Total East Coast gasoline stockpiles dropped 1.915 million barrels week over week to 51.642 million barrels, reversing the prior week's build and leaving regional inventories 3.706 million barrels below year-ago levels.
The drop in foreign supply overwhelmed domestic refining output, which remained subdued due to ongoing autumn turnaround activity. East Coast refinery utilization fell 0.3 percentage point on the week to 80.8%, sitting 12.9 percentage points lower year over year.
Refinery production slipped alongside run rates, with finished gasoline output dipping by 8,000 b/d week over week to 3.138 million b/d, trailing year-ago levels by 73,000 b/d.

With domestic production constrained and waterborne imports falling below the 200,000 b/d threshold for the second time in a month, regional supply failed to keep pace with demand, driving the largest weekly East Coast stock decline in nearly a month.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed 13.5 RVP RBOB New York Harbor barges at NYMEX November RBOB futures plus 3.55 cents/gallon on Oct. 21.
-- Julia Martinez
US DATA: Gasoline exports jump as jet fuel shipments fall, EIA says
Gasoline exports surge 14.5% in latest week
Jet fuel shipments drop 25.9% from prior period
Panama's US product imports soar over 400%: CAS
US gasoline exports in the week ended Oct. 16 rebounded from the previous week's decline, while jet fuel exports declined for the second straight week and diesel exports grew marginally, according to data from the US Energy Information Administration published on Oct. 21.
Total gasoline exports averaged 926,000 barrels/day in the week ended Oct. 16, up 134,000 b/d from the previous week's total.
Additionally, the four-week average dating to the week ended Sept. 25 was 870,250 b/d, down nearly 3.4% from the previous four-week range.
Jet fuel exports averaged 232,000 b/d for the week ended Oct. 16, down 60,000 b/d, or almost 25.9%, from the previous week, EIA data showed. Despite the on-week decline, the most recent four-week export average was 6.4%, or 302,250 b/d, higher.
Diesel exports rose 6,000 b/d to 1.379 million b/d from 1.373 million b/d for the week ended Oct. 9, the EIA data showed.

products expected in the week ending Oct. 24 with 856,100 b/d, according to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data. The volume is more than 400% higher than the 169,400 b/d that landed in the country in the previous week.
Panama is routinely used as a storage hub for products before they reach their intended destination.
Venezuelan imports of US products are set to decline more than 64%, with 43,700 b/d slated to flow into Venzuela in the week ending Oct. 24, while 121,900 b/d arrived in the previous week, according to CAS.

US DATA: West Coast gasoline stocks fall on lower imports, refinery output
Inventories drop 332K barrels in third week of losses
Imports plunge 41K b/d, erase prior gains
Refinery production falls below 1.3M b/d
West Coast gasoline inventories fell for a third consecutive week during the period ended Oct. 16, weighed down by a sharp pullback in imports and sliding refinery output, according to Energy Information Administration data released Oct. 21.
Total gasoline stockpiles decreased by 332,000 barrels week over week to 27.475 million barrels. The drawdown widened the region's annual inventory deficit, leaving stocks 2.036 million barrels below levels from the year-ago week. Regional inventories were last lower four weeks earlier, during the week ended Sept. 18, when stocks stood at 27.328 million barrels.
A decline in foreign arrivals significantly tightened the supply balance. Gasoline imports into the West Coast fell 41,000 b/d week over week to average 55,000 barrels/day, erasing most of the prior week's influx. On an annual basis, import volumes were 129,000 b/d below year-ago levels.
Compounding the drop in imports, regional refinery production contracted further. Finished gasoline output fell 34,000 b/d week over week to average 1.299 million b/d, falling below the 1.3 million b/d mark for the first time in several months. Production trailed year-ago output by 85,000 b/d.
Meanwhile, top-line refinery activity eased for a second straight week. West Coast refinery utilization fell 1.2 percentage points week over week to 92.0%. Despite the weekly pullback, refinery runs remained well above the 2025 pace, 15.7 percentage points above year-ago levels.
Overall, the combination of shrinking finished gasoline yields and a slowdown in waterborne cargoes kept downward pressure on regional stockpiles, keeping West Coast inventories pinned near multi-month lows.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed San Francisco regular CARBOB at NYMEX November RBOB futures plus 35 cents/ gal and Los Angeles CARBOB at futures plus 37 cents/gal on Oct. 21.
-- Julia Martinez
US DATA: ethanol output climbs as blender demand hits six-month low
Output rises to 1.094Mbd
Total inventories rise, anchored by 5% build in Midwest
Ethanol blending inputs hit lowest level since April

US ethanol production rose 2.72% during the week ended Oct. 16, while blender inputs fell to a six-month low, according to Energy Information Administration data released Oct. 21.
Ethanol production rose 29,000 barrels/day to 1.094 million b/d, as output has ramped up following the conclusion of seasonal plant turnarounds. During the previous four reporting weeks, production averaged 1.038 million b/d and reached as low as 1.007 million b/d. Compared to the year-ago week, production was 18,000 b/d, or 1.62%, lower.
Total stocks rose 616,000 barrels, or 2.57%, to 24.558 million barrels. Compared to the year-ago week, total stocks were 2.639 million barrels, or 12.04%, higher. Stock increases were headlined by a significant build in the Midwest, while a draw was reported on the East Coast.
Midwest stocks rose 456,000 barrels to 9.652 million barrels. Compared to the year-ago week, regional stocks were 838,000 barrels, or 9.51%, higher. The Midwest hosts the most liquid trading hub in the US at the Kinder Morgan Inc. terminal near Chicago, as well as most of the ethanol production plants in the US.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, last assessed the Chicago Terminal ethanol benchmark at $2.0480/gal on Oct. 20. Spot prices saw downside pressure this past week as corn markets continued to move lower on heavier-than-expected supply expectations along with harvest progress, while ethanol supply and demand indicators have softened heading into the heart of Q4.
East Coast stocks fell 138,000 barrels to 7.101 million barrels. Compared to the year-ago week, regional stocks were 718,000 barrels, or 11.25%, higher. The East Coast hosts large consumption hubs and the New York Harbor market, a key hub for ethanol trading.
Platts last assessed New York Harbor ITT Sewaren at $2.1975/ gal, while front-month October FOB barges were assessed at $2.1825/gal on Oct. 20.
Gulf Coast stocks rose 193,000 barrels to 5.209 million barrels. Compared to the year-ago week, regional stocks were 1.271 million barrels, or 32.28%, higher. The region is the most common origin for exports, but also hosts large consumption hubs.
Weekly ethanol exports rose 11,000 b/d to 132,000 b/d, according to the EIA. Year-to-date, exports are averaging 146,000 b/d. In 2025, the US set an annual record for ethanol exports and averaged 131,750 b/d per week.
West Coast stocks rose 100,000 barrels to 2.259 million barrels. Compared to the year-ago week, regional stocks were 157,000 barrels, or 6.50%, lower.
The four-week rolling average of refiner and blender net ethanol inputs fell 4,000 b/d to a six-month low of 900,000 b/d. Meanwhile, the weekly average fell 8,000 b/d to 891,000 b/d, also reaching a six-month low. Compared to the year-ago week, the weekly average was 20,000 b/d, or 2.20%, lower.
After hitting a seven-month low the previous week, the four-week rolling average of gasoline demand, represented by product supplied, rose 34,000 b/d to 8.712 million b/d. Meanwhile,

Brazil hydrous ethanol sales rise 16% on year in September: ANP
September volume reaches 2.03 MMcm
Sales post highest September in ANP historical series
Ethanol share of Otto-cycle market rises to 27%
Brazilian hydrous ethanol sales reached 2.03 million cubic meters in September, rising 15.7% year over year and posting the strongest volume for the month in the historical series dating back to 2000, published by the National Agency of Petroleum, Natural Gas and Biofuels, or ANP.
Distributors sold 2.028 MMcm of hydrous ethanol during September, compared with 1.752 MMcm in the same month of 2025, according to preliminary data from ANP released Oct. 21.
September sales eased 1.3% from about 2.055 MMcm in August but remained above 2 MMcm for a second consecutive month.
The latest figures extend a recovery in hydrous ethanol demand seen during the second half of 2026 and provide further evidence of strong consumption as Brazil moves into a seasonally favorable period for the biofuel.
From January through September, hydrous ethanol sales totaled about 16.44 MMcm, up 4.4% from 15.75 MMcm during the corresponding period of 2025.
The strengthening ethanol demand contrasted with a softer broader fuels market. Total Brazilian fuel distribution volumes across retail, business-to-business and reseller segments were around 0.2% lower year over year in September, while Otto-cycle fuel demand increased about 2%, according to an analysis of the ANP data.
Hydrous ethanol was the main driver of the Otto-cycle increase, with ethanol volumes rising around 16% year over year while gasoline declined about 2%. Ethanol's participation in the Otto-cycle market reached 27%, up 3.1 percentage points from a year earlier.
The higher consumption comes as hydrous ethanol continues to offer favorable economics against gasoline in several of Brazil's largest flex-fuel markets, while government measures have sought to preserve the biofuel's competitive tax differential following reductions in federal gasoline taxes.
Demand conditions have also become increasingly relevant to the Center-South supply balance. Cumulative hydrous ethanol production stood at 16.14 MMcm, up 8.7% year over year, but the inventory cushion versus the previous crop has narrowed

substantially from levels observed earlier in the season, data through Sept. 30, released by Brazil's Agriculture Ministry Oct. 15, showed.
The combination of stronger consumption and a narrowing inventory surplus has supported producer sentiment in recent weeks, particularly as repeated rainfall has interrupted cane harvesting and reduced the number of available crushing days in parts of the Center-South.

Platts Asia and Middle Eastern Gasoline Daily Market Analysis
Indonesia seeking 92 RON gasoline
Malaysia gasoline supply healthy
The Asian gasoline complex was expected to be rangebound Oct. 21 amid firm regional demand and as Malaysia's gasoline supplies were heard healthy despite a recent refinery shutdown.
Indonesia was heard seeking up to 1.4 million barrels of 92 RON gasoline for November loading, three sources said.
Malaysia's gasoline supplies were heard healthy, possibly bolstered following state-owned Petronas' takeover of Saudi Aramco's stake in the PRefChem refinery.
In May, Petronas completed the acquisition of Aramco's equity stakes in Pengerang Refining Company Sdn. Bhd. and Pengerang Petrochemical Company Sdn. Bhd. (PRefChem), which became a wholly owned subsidiary of Petronas, the companies said in a statement.
"Malaysia is well supplied, even with limited exports from China," a trade source said.
Malaysia's gasoline supplies were heard healthy despite PRefChem shutting its residue fluid catalytic cracker units, as well as polyethylene and polypropylene production units, following a power outage.
Another trade source said Malaysia's gasoline supplies were stable, noting that the earlier return of the Melaka refinery supported production.
Brokers pegged the front-month FOB Singapore 92 RON gasoline swaps crack spread with Brent swaps in the $19.40$19.45/barrel range at 0300 GMT, up day over day from the Platts assessment of $19.25/b at the Oct. 20 Asian close.

Platts European Gasoline Daily Market Analysis
Seasonal demand drop softens gasoline prices
High freight blocks export arbitrage routes
Naphtha demand from chemicals limits blend value
European gasoline markets softened Oct. 21 amid lower seasonal demand and little export opportunity due to high freight rates and uneconomical arbitrages.
Platts assessed FOB AR Eurobob gasoline barges down $37.50/mt day over day at $1,085.25/mt, while the equivalent cash differential widened $32.75/mt to $57.50/mt.
Blend value remained hard to obtain amid naphtha markets driven by strong demand from the chemical sector.
According to Platts pricing data, the front-month gasolinenaphtha spread inched up $1.25/mt to $147.50/mt on Oct. 21, recovering from a six-month low.
In the Mediterranean, prices also slipped, though by smaller margins. Commenting on recent weakness, one trader source said: "I don't think there's a lot to read into it ... When the numbers get this big, like how they've been, volatility [gets] really high."
Platts assessed FOB Med gasoline cargoes at $1,090.75/ mt on Oct. 21, down $8.50/mt on the day and $89.75/mt week over week.
"Things [are] just softening a bit with the seasonality coming up on us, I think," the trader said.
Platts assessed the front-month FOB AR Eurobob barge at $1,027.75/mt, down $5/mt on the day, with the equivalent crack down 85 cents/b at $18.65/b.
The November/December spread fell $7.50/mt to $35/mt, with December/January down $1/mt at $19/mt.
The November Med/North gasoline differential - the spread between the November FOB Med swaps and the equivalent FOB ARA Eurobob barge - was down 25 cents/mt at a $19.75/mt discount, with the December spread unchanged at a $21.50/mt premium.

Jet
Japan to introduce kerosene import subsidies from Oct. 21 until March 20, 2027
Japan subsidizes kerosene import-wholesale gap
Hokkaido retail kerosene hits record ¥155.7/l
Kerosene imports drop 42.9% YOY

Japan will provide financial subsidies for the difference between kerosene import prices, including surcharge/freight and domestic kerosene wholesale prices, to refiners and oil product importers from Oct. 21, 2026, to March 20, 2027, according to a document distributed by the Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry during an Oct. 20 briefing seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy.
A METI official confirmed the document on Oct. 21. Refiners and oil product importers are required to keep supplying kerosene by the same amount in the same month of the previous year to receive the subsidies, the document showed. They are also required to apply to METI for subsidies and provide import and domestic wholesale prices. The definition of imports, such as the time of unloading or customs clearance, will be determined later, the official told Platts. METI will calculate a monthly average of import and domestic wholesale prices for the subsidies, the official added. Minister of Economy, Trade and Industry Ryosei Akazawa said on Oct. 16 that the government will set up three new policies to stably supply kerosene amid higher domestic kerosene prices. First, the government will request refiners, importers and wholesalers to supply kerosene cargoes by the same amount as the same month of the previous year to consumers or business partners. The second policy is a scheme to help refiners sell kerosene cargoes directly to consumers, and the third is a subsidy for the difference between import and domestic wholesale prices. "METI will likely work out details later on a case-by-case basis, while discussing with refiners or oil product importers," a trader familiar with the matter said.
Record high prices The average weekly retail kerosene price in Hokkaido, Japan's
northernmost region, rose 24.5% year over year to ¥155.7/ liter ($0.98/l) on Oct. 19, hitting a record high for the second consecutive week, while the national average was ¥142.9/l, according to METI data published Oct. 21.
"The announcement of the policy package has taken effect in Hokkaido as a rise in the kerosene price was reduced to ¥0.2/l week over week in comparison to ¥3/l for the previous week,

The average assessment price of kerosene rose 46.3% year over year on Oct. 19 to ¥119,500/kl across the Chiba, Kanagawa, Chukyo and Hanshin regions, according to Platts calculations.
The Japanese Consumers' Cooperative Union in Hokkaido issued a statement Oct. 13 asking the national and Hokkaido governments for an emergency policy to tackle the disruption in kerosene distribution and higher kerosene spot market prices, according to the union website.
The statement said record-high wholesale prices raised the regional retail price above ¥190/l, higher than retail gasoline at about ¥170/l, due to a lack of imports, refiners' control of kerosene supply and a restriction of kerosene supply from other prefectures.
Japan's largest refiner ENEOS Corp. has yet to respond to a request for comment, while Idemitsu Kosan Co. Ltd, the second-largest refiner, was immediately unable to be reached for comment.

Kerosene consumption in Hokkaido was 2.19 million kiloliters in fiscal year 2025-26 (April to March), the largest among all 47 prefectures in the country, according to the Petroleum Association of Japan.
Japan's accumulated imports of kerosene declined 42.9% year over year to 177,312 kiloliters from April to August, the latest month available, and kerosene inventories in the country and Hokkaido fell 16.7% and 17.4% year over year to 2.12 million kiloliters and 699,300 kiloliters, respectively, at the end of August, according to METI.

Japan cuts fuel subsidies by 0.5% for Oct. 22-28 on weaker crude
Subsidy down ¥0.20/liter at ¥42.80/liter
Gasoline pump prices rise to ¥170.20/liter
Japan cut its fuel subsidy for oil products to ¥42.80/liter (27 cents/liter) for the week of Oct. 22-28, from ¥43/liter the previous week, amid weaker crude oil prices, the government said Oct. 21.
The government calculates the weekly subsidy using data that includes the average retail gasoline price, as surveyed every Monday, crude oil prices and other factors. It provides a full

subsidy for the portion exceeding ¥170/liter ($1.08/liter). The national average retail price for regular gasoline
rose ¥0.10/liter week over week to ¥170.20/liter as of Oct. 19, according to the Oil Information Center of the Institute of Energy Economics, Japan.
The Ministry of Economy, Trade and Industry used Nikkei Dubai as the benchmark for calculating subsidies from Jan. 27, 2022, to March 18, 2026, and ICE Brent from March 19 to June 3.
METI informed trading houses and wholesalers in a May 27 email, seen by Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, that the subsidy amount would be calculated using Platts Dubai as the benchmark, starting with payments made June 4.
The Platts front-month cash Dubai assessment fell 0.5% week over week to $107.59/barrel over Oct. 13-19.
In March, due to an increase in crude oil prices driven by the Middle East conflict, the government reinstated gasoline subsidies to maintain the retail price of regular gasoline at ¥170/ liter after ending them on Dec. 30, 2025, according to documents released by METI on March 11.
A fixed subsidy of ¥5/liter was provided for kerosene and fuel oil and was scheduled to end on March 31. However, the government has been providing these fuels with the same subsidy as gasoline since March 19.
For gasoil, the provisional tax rate of ¥17.10/liter was abolished on April 1, according to METI. To prevent demand fluctuations caused by the price drop from the tax abolition, METI said Nov. 7, 2025, that it would provide a subsidy of ¥17.10/liter, equal to the provisional tax rate, from Nov. 27, 2025, to March 31, 2026.
Since March 19, when the gasoline subsidy was reinstated, gasoil has received a subsidy of ¥17.10/liter, the same as gasoline. From April 1, the gasoil subsidy was set at the same level as the gasoline subsidy, according to METI.
Japan launched the subsidy program for gasoline, kerosene, gasoil and fuel oil in January 2022 to mitigate the impact of high oil prices on the economy as it recovered from the pandemic. In April 2022, the program was expanded to include jet fuel and LPG.
Refiners use the subsidy to curb increases in the weekly wholesale prices of oil products, while trading houses deduct it from the selling prices of imported oil products.
The Platts-assessed average prices across the Chiba, Kanagawa, Chukyo, and Hanshin regions on Oct. 21 are as follows: gasoline at ¥99,825/kiloliter ($100.39/b), kerosene at ¥119,500/ kl, gasoil at ¥100,600/kl, low-sulfur A-fuel oil at ¥99,800/kl and A-fuel oil at ¥99,300/kl.
-- Hiroyuki Koshoji

In related news, Europe's largest airline, Ryanair Holdings Plc, sees ticket costs soaring by 10%-20% in 2027 as companies near a cliff-edge on their oil price hedges, CEO Michael O'Leary said Oct. 20.

In the past year, the sector has already been subject to a wave of recent consolidation, most recently through Lufthansa's recent acquisition of stakes in Italy's ITA and Portugal's TAP.
In response to higher national aviation taxes, Ryanair itself is canceling 20 routes to and from Belgium this winter, the budget air carrier announced Oct. 20, while sector-wide bankruptcies and takeovers could also limit the number of seats available next year, O'Leary said.

Platts European Jet Daily Market Analysis
Refineries maximize jet output above diesel levels: sources
Airlines brace for high fuel costs through 2028
The European jet fuel market softened Oct. 21 as higher seasonal demand approached its end, with supply tightness persisting in the market.
Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed the Jet CIF NWE Cargo price at $1,594.00/metric ton, down $33/mt day over day. The Jet FOB FARAG barge price also decreased $27.5/mt, closing at $ 1,619.75/mt. The October ICE LSGO contract fell $18.75/ mt day over day to $1,431.25/mt, with the October/November backwardation widening $3.50/mt to $52.25/mt.
Several European refineries have already transitioned to jet fuel production this month, aiming to maximize output, according to market sources. "The arbitrage to Europe is open, and jet fuel prices continue to trade above diesel," a trading source said.
The benchmark European jet fuel physical crack stood at $85.36/b Oct. 20, according to Platts data, $13.78 above the physical Amsterdam-Rotterdam-Antwerp diesel crack.
Demand for jet fuel across Europe remained seasonally robust during the August-October period, with the Mediterranean region particularly active. "With November approaching, we anticipate a seasonal slowdown until the Christmas holidays, which may offer some relief, as supply remains very tight," another trader noted. Industry discussions have focused on the significant spike in airfares expected next year, with this trend observed across multiple regions.
WestJet Group is preparing for elevated aviation fuel costs through 2027 and potentially into 2028, according to the company's CEO. Meanwhile, TAP Air Portugal has cautioned that prolonged price pressures could undermine airline profitability and hasten industry consolidation, even as Latin American travel demand continues to show resilience. The comments were made by the carriers' CEOs during the ALTA Airline Leaders Forum, held Oct. 18-20 in Mexico City.
At the ALTA Airline Leaders Forum in the Dominican Republic, Manuel Luna, director general of communications and external affairs for Dominican airline Arajet, stated that the sharp increase in fuel expenses has compelled the airline to revise its pricing strategy.

Diesel
Russia shipping first diesel cargo to US this week after sanctions waiver
First US-bound diesel tanker leaving Oct. 22
Russia targets 500,000 mt US shipments by Nov
Refinery attacks limit export capacity recovery
Russia is set to dispatch its first diesel tanker to the US on Oct. 22 after the Trump administration issued sweeping sanctions waivers aimed at reopening trade flows, according to Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak.
The first tanker, which has a displacement of some 40,000 mt, making it medium-range in scale, will depart from Russia's Baltic coastline, Novak told local media, adding that subsequent batches will follow later. It will be the first shipment of its kind to leave for the US since January 2022, before the US first sanctioned Russian energy flows.
Historically, the world's second-largest exporter of seaborne diesel, Russia still faces restrictions on its capacity due to Ukrainian attacks on its refineries, which have been a focal point for targeted offensives. However, the country has taken steps to partly relax a diesel export ban intended to conserve its domestic supply, in a concession to Trump for easing sanctions.
In his comments to national press, Novak said Russia is not yet restoring its diesel exports in full, but will "within the abilities of [its] refineries." Toward the end of the year, Russia can restore its full diesel export volumes, and broaden its shipments to other countries, he said.

The deputy prime minister said earlier this week that Russia typically consumes 3.5 - 4 million metric tons/month diesel and exports 3.5 million mt/month, a figure he said the country can reach again by the year-end. Novak previously said that Russia is ready to supply 300,000 mt of diesel to the US in October, and a further 500,000 mt in November.
According to S&P Global Commodities at Sea data, the country has shipped around 400,000 mt of diesel internationally to date in October, with most of it traveling to Egypt, Morocco, Turkey and Brazil. In the week following the US announcement, exports more than doubled, Platts, part of S&P Global, reported previously.
Based on the latest prewar data, Russia produced around 7.5 million mt/month of diesel, although repeated hits to its refining sector have hamstrung production. In the lead-up to his authorization of Russian diesel imports, Trump repeatedly blamed Ukraine's military tactics for high diesel prices -- a key policy concern for Trump ahead of the US midterm elections.
The US Congress banned Russian energy imports with its Ending Importation of Russian Oil Act in April 2022 in response to the country's full-scale invasion of Ukraine. Prior to the conflict, most of Russia's diesel went to Europe, while shipments to the US were only nominal at 100,000 - 200,000 mt/month. In contrast, the US has typically relied on Northwest Europe, Canada and South Korea as lead suppliers of foreign diesel.
Price focus Officials from the White House and Moscow have said the
move will ease pressure on global markets, which continue to face prices close to record highs due to the Middle East conflict and loss of Russian supply. Russia's President Vladimir Putin said at a meeting with US envoy Steve Witkoff Oct. 21 that after the announcement of new exports to the US, prices fell by 5-6% on the international market, according to the transcripts of the meeting published on the Kremlin website.
Putin also confirmed that Russia is getting several tankers ready to leave and the first tanker will leave "in two-three days, 40,000 mt" with other tankers following thereafter. "The key now is to ensure the financial side of things is in place so that everything functions as intended," he added.
However, traders and commentators have noted that the volumes under discussion represent just a small fraction of US import needs, and could struggle to move the needle on international prices.
"The amount of diesel Russia can provide would be a drop in the bucket compared to global demand," said Masha Kotkin, a fellow at the Center for Strategic and International Studies. "If you are asking about US diesel prices, I don't think it's going to make a difference. It is not very much in the scheme of things," she said.
Despite intense focus from the White House on bringing down costs, distillate benchmarks have continued to hover around record levels. ICE Low Sulfur Gasoil futures settled at $1,449.5/ mt on Oct. 20, up from $1,420.75/mt on Oct. 9 and close to September's all-time high of $1,478.25/mt.
-- Elza Turner, Kelly Norways

US DATA: Gulf Coast ULSD stocks swell on strong production
Gulf Coast ULSD production hits 29-week high
Midwest diesel stocks drop to a 19-week low
Chicago ULSD prices surge to monthly highs
US nationwide ultra-low sulfur diesel stocks grew by 1.3 million barrels to 92.88 million barrels in the week ended Oct. 16, but the build masks a regional divergence with a large build on the Gulf Coast and a tightening market in the Midwest, according to Energy Information Administration data released Oct. 21.
The largest inventory buildup came from the Gulf Coast, where stocks increased by 2.17 million barrels to 36.24 million barrels, its highest level since the week ended July 24. The build was influenced by a rise in regional production, which climbed 54,000 b/d to 2.95 million b/d, a 29-week high.
In contrast, the Midwest saw stocks fall by 421,000 barrels to 24.48 million barrels, a 19-week low. The draw sent prices in the region to their highest levels in a month. Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, assessed Chicago generic ULSD Oct. 21 up 48.83 cents/gallon to $5.363/gal on Oct. 21, its highest level since Sept. 18.
Nationwide, US ULSD production rose 65,000 b/d to 5.01 million b/d, climbing back above the symbolic 5 million b/d level to a two-week high.
The stock build occurred even as exports ticked higher, rising 6,000 b/d to 1.379 million b/d. While only a two-week high, export levels remain well above the five-year average of 1.037 million b/d.
Meanwhile, product supplied, a proxy for demand, fell by 591,000 b/d to 3.73 million b/d, a two-week low. The decline follows a 29-week high seen in the prior reporting week.
-- Carlos Castillo
US offers emergency heating oil loan as winter fuel costs surge
DOE offers 315,000 barrels from oil reserve
Distillate prices surge ahead of winter season
Global diesel exports drop 700,000 b/d yearly: IEA
The US Department of Energy is offering to loan up to 315,000 barrels from the Northeast Home Heating Oil Reserve, with deliveries scheduled to be completed by Nov. 13, as distillate markets face elevated prices ahead of winter, the agency announced Oct. 21.
The offer would cover supplies in Maine, Massachusetts and Connecticut and represent nearly one-third of the 1 million-barrel NHHOR emergency stockpile. In an email, a DOE spokesperson did not comment on the potential for additional future releases. The measure is "structured to mitigate supply chain challenges disrupting fuel oil supply," DOE wrote in its release.

"The Trump Administration is committed to doing everything in our power to lower energy costs for the American people," US Energy Secretary Chris Wright said in a statement.
The heating oil offer follows DOE's September solicitation for an exchange of up to 40 million barrels of sour crude from the US Strategic Petroleum Reserve, part of a US commitment to release 172 million barrels under the International Energy Agency's coordinated emergency stock release announced in March, shortly after the US-Israel war on Iran disrupted Middle East trade flows and roiled global energy markets.
Under SPR exchanges, companies may borrow crude and subsequently return the volume along with additional barrels as a premium, rather than purchasing the oil outright. On Sept. 23, US Energy Secretary Chris Wright told a Dow Jones event the SPR would be refilled with "roughly 1.25 barrels" for every barrel it released during the 2026 exchanges.
DOE said the quantities available for exchange were a minimum of 25,000 barrels and a maximum of 30,000.
Distillate prices The heating oil announcement comes as international efforts
to increase diesel availability continue. IEA members plan to release more than 20 million barrels of diesel as soon as possible, Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, reported Oct. 15. The agency did not specify the timing or geographical distribution of those releases.
Platts assessed benchmark European diesel, ULSD 10ppm sulfur CIF Northwest Europe cargoes, at $1,470.5/mt Oct. 21, down from a high $1,642.25/mt Sept. 15. Platts Dated Brent was assessed at $118.13/b Oct. 21.
US retail diesel prices averaged $6.276/gal Oct. 19, up 9.7 cents/gal from the previous week and $2.656/gal from a year earlier, according to the US Energy Information Administration. New England diesel averaged $6.470/gal, up 2.9 cents/gal on the week.
The IEA said in its October oil market report that global diesel exports in September were 700,000 b/d below year-earlier levels. Reduced Russian and Middle Eastern shipments were only partly offset by increased supplies elsewhere, as prolonged disruptions prompted counter-seasonal inventory draws ahead of autumn refinery maintenance and peak winter demand.
"Of course, this is a temporary measure to solve a temporary supply disruption," Wright said in a statement. "I encourage the governors of New York and New England to work with the Trump Administration to build the Constitution Pipeline and replace home heating oil with low-cost natural gas."
On Oct. 19, Wright unveiled DOE plans to spend up to $42 million in Defense Production Act funding to support Williams Cos. Inc.'s Constitution Pipeline project, a 125-mile pipeline that would move gas from production areas in Susquehanna County, Pennsylvania, to an interconnect with Iroquois Gas Transmission in Schoharie County, New York.
-- Eamonn Brennan

October 21, 2026

South Korea rejects calls for higher diesel exports, but refiners work to meet global demand
Export volumes remain capped at 2025 levels
Refiners receive strong demand for more spot cargoes
Seoul uses MOPS for domestic price cap policy
The South Korean government is holding firm on ensuring adequate domestic supplies of clean oil products by capping exports at 2025 levels, rejecting the refining sector's proposal to increase overseas sales, although local refiners remain committed to meeting strong global demand as much as possible, according to the trade ministry and industry sources.
South Korea -- Asia's top supplier and net exporter of transportation fuels and middle distillates -- has met its contractual supply obligations to term customers across the Asia-Pacific region so far this year. The government and refiners' trading teams have worked together to secure sufficient feedstock crude through supply diversification and alternative logistical strategies, product managers at major refiners in Seosan, Ulsan and Seoul told Platts, part of S&P Global Energy, during market discussions over Oct. 19-21.
However, demand for additional spot supplies of clean oil products from South Korean refiners has surged, particularly for diesel, with the Asian crack spread for the essential middle distillate reaching record highs in the second half of 2026, according to the product managers and the Ministry of Trade, Industry and Resources.
The second-month Singapore gasoil swap crack against Dubai crude swaps reached a record $83.02/barrel on Sept. 16. It averaged $72.97/b in September, compared with $64.71/b in August and the 2025 average of $18.16/b, Platts data showed. The crack spread was last assessed at $66.91/b on Oct. 20.

effective closure of the Strait of Hormuz. Under the measures, refiners cannot export more of
the controlled products in any month than they did in the corresponding month of 2025. They must also maintain monthly domestic supplies at more than 90% of the same month's sales volume a year earlier, according to MOTIR.
"Amid lingering instability in the international supply of diesel and geopolitical risks, securing a stable [domestic] supply of diesel remains the top priority," a MOTIR official said, citing the fuel's importance to domestic industry and household livelihoods.
Still, the ceiling is not entirely inflexible. The government may approve exports exceeding 100% of year-earlier volumes after consulting refiners, provided market and domestic inventory conditions permit, MOTIR said.
Strong margins, limited flexibility The restrictions constrain refiners' ability to fully capitalize
on exceptionally strong regional margins. Reduced Chinese fuel exports, geopolitical disruptions and Ukrainian strikes on Russian refineries have tightened diesel availability, prompting buyers across Asia, Oceania, Europe and the US West Coast to seek additional spot supplies from South Korea, the product managers said.
Apart from gasoil/diesel, Platts assessed the FOB Singapore second-month jet fuel/kerosene crack spread against Dubai swaps at an average of $49.17/b so far in 2026, more than double the 2025 average of $17.32/b.

Seoul recently rejected refiners' requests to ease export controls, emphasizing that maintaining stable domestic supplies and preventing sharp increases in pump prices remained its priorities, MOTIR said in a statement Oct. 19.
The government imposed restrictions on exports of gasoline, diesel and jet fuel/kerosene on March 13 amid concerns over supply disruptions following the US-Israel war with Iran and the

South Korea nevertheless remains well-positioned to meet its term commitments. Refiners have secured steady supplies of light sweet US crude, heavy sour Canadian barrels, Latin American grades and staple Middle Eastern sour crude.
South Korea has about 3.4 million barrels/day of refining capacity, compared with domestic oil demand of roughly 2.3 million b/d, leaving substantial capacity available for exports after domestic requirements are met.

Stable domestic retail prices The export controls are intended to help ensure
adequate domestic supplies and support the government's fuel price ceiling, introduced on March 13 to limit increases in domestic gasoline, diesel and kerosene prices, MOTIR said.
Maximum prices are calculated using average domestic supply costs, changes in Mean of Platts Singapore assessments and applicable taxes. The government will compensate refiners for losses incurred under the program after it ends and will lift the price ceiling and export restrictions once international oil markets stabilize, MOTIR added.
Together with fuel tax reductions, the measures helped bring average retail diesel prices down to 1,843 won/liter ($1.28/liter) in the second week of October, down 8.1% from 2,006 won/liter five months earlier, according to MOTIR. Gasoline prices fell 7.7% to 1,857 won/liter over the same period.
